========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, hi. I’m here because… well, what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that lately my mind just will not stay on one thing. I drive for work, deliveries and sales calls, that kind of thing, and over these past months it’s like every little thing grabs me — the CB crackling, a fuel price sign changing, some new idea popping into my head — and then I’m off track.

And I’m not usually like that, or at least not to where it causes problems. I’ve been missing details I normally catch, like a client’s delivery note, or I’ll forget who I told I’d call back. Then I’m sitting there later trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s frustrating because I’ve done this work a long time. It’s not like I don’t know how to keep track of things. My attention just keeps jumping around, and that’s what got me worried enough to come in.

Yeah, I do. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m pacing around every second, but I definitely have this… antsy feeling if I’m just sitting too long. I’m not sure I’d call it impulsive, exactly. I mean, I can usually think things through when it matters, like with driving or dealing with customers. I’m not out there just doing wild things without any thought.

Sometimes I’ll jump into something around the house or at work before really sitting down and deciding if it needs doing, but that feels more like I’m already keyed up and I just start moving, if that makes sense. Like I’ll decide something has to be handled right then, and later I might think, “Well, that could’ve waited.” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by acting on impulse. I haven’t really noticed myself making big reckless decisions or anything like that.

If I sit still, I start feeling keyed up, like I need to get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it impulsive, exactly. I mean, I can usually think things through when it matters, like with driving or dealing with customers. I’m not out there just doing wild things without any thought.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump into something around the house or at work before really sitting down and deciding if it needs doing, but that feels more like I’m already keyed up and I just start moving, if that makes sense. Like I’ll decide something has to be handled right then, and later I might think, “Well, that could’ve waited.” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by acting on impulse. I haven’t really noticed myself making big reckless decisions or anything like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 50 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that, honestly. I mean, I can talk if I’m comfortable with people, like at work or with family, and sometimes I probably explain things more than I need to — my wife has told me before I can take the long way around a story — but I wouldn’t say I’m always the one dominating a conversation or talking over everybody.

In social situations, it kind of depends who I’m with. If it’s people I know, I’ll join in, sure. If it’s people I don’t know well, I’m not usually trying to be the center of attention or anything. So… I guess I’m not sure I’d say “more than others,” no. At least it’s not something that’s stood out to me as a problem.

I don’t think so… not in a way that people have pointed out to me, anyway. I mean, everybody cuts in now and then when a conversation’s moving fast, especially at work when there’s a schedule or someone’s trying to explain a route and I already know the part they’re getting to. But I wouldn’t describe it as me barging over people all the time or not letting anyone talk. It’s more… if I’m interested or I think there’s something that needs to be clarified, I might come in a little early. But I haven’t really noticed it as a major problem.

I mean, everybody cuts in now and then when a conversation’s moving fast, especially at work when there’s a schedule or someone’s trying to explain a route and I already know the part they’re getting to. I might jump in with, “Yeah, yeah, I’ve got it,” or ask a question before they’re totally finished, but I don’t think I’m constantly interrupting people.\n\nMy wife might say I get ahead of myself sometimes, like I think I know what she’s about to say and I answer that instead of letting her finish. I haven’t really noticed myself making big reckless decisions or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that, honestly. I mean, I can talk if I’m comfortable with people, like at work or with family, and sometimes I probably explain things more than I need to — my wife has told me before I can take the long way around a story — but I wouldn’t say I’m always the one dominating a conversation or talking over everybody.\n\nIn social situations, it kind of depends who I’m with. If it’s people I know, I’ll join in, sure. If it’s people I don’t know well, I’m not usually trying to be the center of attention or anything. So… I guess I’m not sure I’d say “more than others,” no. At least it’s not something that’s stood out to me as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that people have pointed out to me, anyway. I mean, everybody cuts in now and then when a conversation’s moving fast, especially at work when there’s a schedule or someone’s trying to explain a route and I already know the part they’re getting to. I might jump in with, “Yeah, yeah, I’ve got it,” or ask a question before they’re totally finished, but I don’t think I’m constantly interrupting people.\n\nMy wife might say I get ahead of myself sometimes, like I think I know what she’s about to say and I answer that instead of letting her finish. But I wouldn’t describe it as me barging over people all the time or not letting anyone talk. It’s more… if I’m interested or I think there’s something that needs to be clarified, I might come in a little early. But I haven’t really noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your attention and feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about seven months now, give or take. It started around the time my daughter moved back home, and I was trying to keep everything steady — the house, work, everybody’s routine, all of that. At first I thought it was just stress from the change, like I had more on my plate and needed to stay on top of things, but it’s kind of kept going.\n\nSo… yeah, roughly seven months. It wasn’t something I noticed overnight, more like I looked back and realized I’d been feeling keyed up and having trouble keeping my attention where it needed to be for a while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say no, but honestly it has started to cause some issues. Not like I’ve blown up my job or anything, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.

At work, with driving and sales, there are a lot of little details you’re supposed to keep straight — addresses, delivery notes, what dispatch said, what a client asked for, who needed a call back, that kind of thing. Lately I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat things I really should’ve caught the first time, and sometimes clients too, which is embarrassing because I don’t want to come across like I’m not listening. A couple of sales follow-ups slipped through the cracks because I was trying to chase too many things at once in my head, like I’d think of one idea, then another, then I’d tell myself I’d circle back… and then I didn’t.

And at home, yeah, it’s causing strain. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least that’s how it feels. I can see them measuring what they say or trying not to set me off or add to my stress, and that makes me feel awful because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady at home, not making everybody tense. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting both work and relationships some. Not completely out of control, but it’s there.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. I’m here because… well, what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that lately my mind just will not stay on one thing. I drive for work, deliveries and sales calls, that kind of thing, and over these past months it’s like every little thing grabs me — the CB crackling, a fuel price sign changing, some new idea popping into my head — and then I’m off track.\n\nAnd I’m not usually like that, or at least not to where it causes problems. I’ve been missing details I normally catch, like a client’s delivery note, or I’ll forget who I told I’d call back. Then I’m sitting there later trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s frustrating because I’ve done this work a long time. It’s not like I don’t know how to keep track of things. My attention just keeps jumping around, and that’s what got me worried enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety, like you're constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m pacing around every second, but I definitely have this… antsy feeling if I’m just sitting too long. Like if I’m home and there’s nothing I’m supposed to be doing, I’ll suddenly decide the garage needs fixing up, or something around the house has to be tightened or cleaned or reorganized right then.\n\nAnd with work, I keep adding things for myself that probably don’t need to be done. I’ll rewrite my route plan even after it’s already fine, or check in on newer drivers after hours, like I’ve appointed myself to make sure everything’s running right. If I sit still, I start feeling keyed up, like I need to get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it impulsive, exactly. It wasn’t something I noticed overnight, more like I looked back and realized I’d been feeling keyed up and having trouble keeping my attention where it needed to be for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with your attention and restlessness been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say no, but honestly it has started to cause some issues. Not like I’ve blown up my job or anything, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nAt work, with driving and sales, there are a lot of little details you’re supposed to keep straight — addresses, delivery notes, what dispatch said, what a client asked for, who needed a call back, that kind of thing. Lately I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat things I really should’ve caught the first time, and sometimes clients too, which is embarrassing because I don’t want to come across like I’m not listening. A couple of sales follow-ups slipped through the cracks because I was trying to chase too many things at once in my head, like I’d think of one idea, then another, then I’d tell myself I’d circle back… and then I didn’t.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s causing strain. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least that’s how it feels. I can see them measuring what they say or trying not to set me off or add to my stress, and that makes me feel awful because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady at home, not making everybody tense. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting both work and relationships some. Not completely out of control, but it’s there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not officially, no. I haven’t cut my hours or had my responsibilities taken away or anything like that. I’m still doing the driving and the sales side of things.

But I’ll be honest, it’s made the work feel harder than it should. I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat details I normally would’ve tracked the first time — addresses, timing, little instructions — and that bothers me because I take pride in being reliable. Same with clients, there’ve been moments where I’m thinking, “Come on, you should’ve caught that,” and then I have to ask again.

And a couple of sales follow-ups slipped through because I had too many things bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call this person back,” then another idea or task would jump in, and by the time I circled back, I’d missed it or delayed it. So no, I haven’t reduced my workload formally, but I can feel myself needing more effort just to keep up.

At home it’s kind of similar in a different way. My wife and daughter haven’t told me, “You need to do less,” exactly, but I can tell they’re walking on eggshells sometimes, like they’re trying not to add to my stress. That makes me feel bad, because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady, not making the house feel tense.

I’ve been missing details I normally catch, like a client’s delivery note, or I’ll forget who I told I’d call back. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m pacing around every second, but I definitely have this… antsy feeling if I’m just sitting too long. I’ll rewrite my route plan even after it’s already fine, or check in on newer drivers after hours, like I’ve appointed myself to make sure everything’s running right. At least it’s not something that’s stood out to me as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that people have pointed out to me, anyway. I mean, everybody cuts in now and then when a conversation’s moving fast, especially at work when there’s a schedule or someone’s trying to explain a route and I already know the part they’re getting to. I might jump in with, “Yeah, yeah, I’ve got it,” or ask a question before they’re totally finished, but I don’t think I’m constantly interrupting people.\n\nMy wife might say I get ahead of myself sometimes, like I think I know what she’s about to say and I answer that instead of letting her finish. But I wouldn’t describe it as me barging over people all the time or not letting anyone talk. It’s more… if I’m interested or I think there’s something that needs to be clarified, I might come in a little early. But I haven’t really noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your attention and feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about seven months now, give or take. It started around the time my daughter moved back home, and I was trying to keep everything steady — the house, work, everybody’s routine, all of that. At first I thought it was just stress from the change, like I had more on my plate and needed to stay on top of things, but it’s kind of kept going.\n\nSo… yeah, roughly seven months. It wasn’t something I noticed overnight, more like I looked back and realized I’d been feeling keyed up and having trouble keeping my attention where it needed to be for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with your attention and restlessness been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say no, but honestly it has started to cause some issues. Not like I’ve blown up my job or anything, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nAt work, with driving and sales, there are a lot of little details you’re supposed to keep straight — addresses, delivery notes, what dispatch said, what a client asked for, who needed a call back, that kind of thing. Lately I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat things I really should’ve caught the first time, and sometimes clients too, which is embarrassing because I don’t want to come across like I’m not listening. A couple of sales follow-ups slipped through the cracks because I was trying to chase too many things at once in my head, like I’d think of one idea, then another, then I’d tell myself I’d circle back… and then I didn’t.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s causing strain. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least that’s how it feels. I can see them measuring what they say or trying not to set me off or add to my stress, and that makes me feel awful because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady at home, not making everybody tense. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting both work and relationships some. Not completely out of control, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to reduce your work hours or responsibilities due to these difficulties with attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not officially, no. I haven’t cut my hours or had my responsibilities taken away or anything like that. I’m still doing the driving and the sales side of things.\n\nBut I’ll be honest, it’s made the work feel harder than it should. I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat details I normally would’ve tracked the first time — addresses, timing, little instructions — and that bothers me because I take pride in being reliable. Same with clients, there’ve been moments where I’m thinking, “Come on, you should’ve caught that,” and then I have to ask again.\n\nAnd a couple of sales follow-ups slipped through because I had too many things bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call this person back,” then another idea or task would jump in, and by the time I circled back, I’d missed it or delayed it. So no, I haven’t reduced my workload formally, but I can feel myself needing more effort just to keep up.\n\nAt home it’s kind of similar in a different way. My wife and daughter haven’t told me, “You need to do less,” exactly, but I can tell they’re walking on eggshells sometimes, like they’re trying not to add to my stress. That makes me feel bad, because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady, not making the house feel tense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yeah… it has, though I’d say it’s more of a steady wearing-down kind of impact than one big dramatic thing. I’m still getting through the day, still going to work, still doing what I’m supposed to do, but it takes more out of me than it used to.

At work, it’s the little stuff that adds up. I’ve had dispatch give me details — like timing, addresses, what the customer said — and then I have to ask them to repeat it because it just didn’t stick the way it normally would. Same with clients sometimes, and I hate that because I don’t want them thinking I’m careless or not paying attention. And then on the sales side, I’ve had a couple follow-ups slip through because I had too many ideas or tasks bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call that person back,” and then something else would grab my attention, and then later I’d realize I never did it. That affects how I feel about myself, because I’ve always tried to be dependable.

At home it’s probably the part that bothers me more. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least I can tell they’re being careful. Like they’re trying not to add pressure or say the wrong thing. And that makes the house feel strained. I’m supposed to be helping, you know, keeping things steady, not being another source of tension.

So yeah, my overall quality of life is affected. Not like I can’t function, but things feel harder, and I don’t feel as easy to be around as I’d like to be.

I’ve had dispatch give me details — like timing, addresses, what the customer said — and then I have to ask them to repeat it because it just didn’t stick the way it normally would. And then on the sales side, I’ve had a couple follow-ups slip through because I had too many ideas or tasks bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call that person back,” and then something else would grab my attention, and then later I’d realize I never did it. That affects how I feel about myself, because I’ve always tried to be dependable.\n\nAt home it’s probably the part that bothers me more. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least I can tell they’re being careful. Like they’re trying not to add pressure or say the wrong thing. And that makes the house feel strained. I’m supposed to be helping, you know, keeping things steady, not being another source of tension.\n\nSo yeah, my overall quality of life is affected. Not like I can’t function, but things feel harder, and I don’t feel as easy to be around as I’d like to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yeah, I’d say it happens in both places, but it shows up a little differently. At work it’s more obvious because there are consequences right away — like I’m driving, I hear every CB chirp, I notice every fuel price sign, some new idea pops into my head, and then suddenly I’ve missed a detail I normally wouldn’t miss. That’s honestly part of what finally got me in here, because over these months it’s been happening enough that I can’t just brush it off.

At home, it’s not like delivery notes or dispatch instructions, but my mind still jumps around. I’ll be trying to listen or do one thing, and then I’m pulled off by something else in my head or around me. So yes, it’s there at home too — maybe work makes it stand out more because there are so many moving parts and little details I’m supposed to track. But it’s not only a work thing.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. I’m here because… well, what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that lately my mind just will not stay on one thing. I drive for work, deliveries and sales calls, that kind of thing, and over these past months it’s like every little thing grabs me — the CB crackling, a fuel price sign changing, some new idea popping into my head — and then I’m off track.\n\nAnd I’m not usually like that, or at least not to where it causes problems. I’ve been missing details I normally catch, like a client’s delivery note, or I’ll forget who I told I’d call back. Then I’m sitting there later trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s frustrating because I’ve done this work a long time. It’s not like I don’t know how to keep track of things. My attention just keeps jumping around, and that’s what got me worried enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety, like you're constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m pacing around every second, but I definitely have this… antsy feeling if I’m just sitting too long. Like if I’m home and there’s nothing I’m supposed to be doing, I’ll suddenly decide the garage needs fixing up, or something around the house has to be tightened or cleaned or reorganized right then.\n\nAnd with work, I keep adding things for myself that probably don’t need to be done. I’ll rewrite my route plan even after it’s already fine, or check in on newer drivers after hours, like I’ve appointed myself to make sure everything’s running right. It feels productive at the time, but it’s also like I can’t just leave things alone. If I sit still, I start feeling keyed up, like I need to get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it impulsive, exactly. I mean, I can usually think things through when it matters, like with driving or dealing with customers. I’m not out there just doing wild things without any thought.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump into something around the house or at work before really sitting down and deciding if it needs doing, but that feels more like I’m already keyed up and I just start moving, if that makes sense. Like I’ll decide something has to be handled right then, and later I might think, “Well, that could’ve waited.” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by acting on impulse. I haven’t really noticed myself making big reckless decisions or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that, honestly. I mean, I can talk if I’m comfortable with people, like at work or with family, and sometimes I probably explain things more than I need to — my wife has told me before I can take the long way around a story — but I wouldn’t say I’m always the one dominating a conversation or talking over everybody.\n\nIn social situations, it kind of depends who I’m with. If it’s people I know, I’ll join in, sure. If it’s people I don’t know well, I’m not usually trying to be the center of attention or anything. So… I guess I’m not sure I’d say “more than others,” no. At least it’s not something that’s stood out to me as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that people have pointed out to me, anyway. I mean, everybody cuts in now and then when a conversation’s moving fast, especially at work when there’s a schedule or someone’s trying to explain a route and I already know the part they’re getting to. I might jump in with, “Yeah, yeah, I’ve got it,” or ask a question before they’re totally finished, but I don’t think I’m constantly interrupting people.\n\nMy wife might say I get ahead of myself sometimes, like I think I know what she’s about to say and I answer that instead of letting her finish. But I wouldn’t describe it as me barging over people all the time or not letting anyone talk. It’s more… if I’m interested or I think there’s something that needs to be clarified, I might come in a little early. But I haven’t really noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your attention and feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about seven months now, give or take. It started around the time my daughter moved back home, and I was trying to keep everything steady — the house, work, everybody’s routine, all of that. At first I thought it was just stress from the change, like I had more on my plate and needed to stay on top of things, but it’s kind of kept going.\n\nSo… yeah, roughly seven months. It wasn’t something I noticed overnight, more like I looked back and realized I’d been feeling keyed up and having trouble keeping my attention where it needed to be for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with your attention and restlessness been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say no, but honestly it has started to cause some issues. Not like I’ve blown up my job or anything, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nAt work, with driving and sales, there are a lot of little details you’re supposed to keep straight — addresses, delivery notes, what dispatch said, what a client asked for, who needed a call back, that kind of thing. Lately I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat things I really should’ve caught the first time, and sometimes clients too, which is embarrassing because I don’t want to come across like I’m not listening. A couple of sales follow-ups slipped through the cracks because I was trying to chase too many things at once in my head, like I’d think of one idea, then another, then I’d tell myself I’d circle back… and then I didn’t.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s causing strain. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least that’s how it feels. I can see them measuring what they say or trying not to set me off or add to my stress, and that makes me feel awful because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady at home, not making everybody tense. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting both work and relationships some. Not completely out of control, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to reduce your work hours or responsibilities due to these difficulties with attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not officially, no. I haven’t cut my hours or had my responsibilities taken away or anything like that. I’m still doing the driving and the sales side of things.\n\nBut I’ll be honest, it’s made the work feel harder than it should. I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat details I normally would’ve tracked the first time — addresses, timing, little instructions — and that bothers me because I take pride in being reliable. Same with clients, there’ve been moments where I’m thinking, “Come on, you should’ve caught that,” and then I have to ask again.\n\nAnd a couple of sales follow-ups slipped through because I had too many things bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call this person back,” then another idea or task would jump in, and by the time I circled back, I’d missed it or delayed it. So no, I haven’t reduced my workload formally, but I can feel myself needing more effort just to keep up.\n\nAt home it’s kind of similar in a different way. My wife and daughter haven’t told me, “You need to do less,” exactly, but I can tell they’re walking on eggshells sometimes, like they’re trying not to add to my stress. That makes me feel bad, because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady, not making the house feel tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily activities or overall quality of life due to these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, though I’d say it’s more of a steady wearing-down kind of impact than one big dramatic thing. I’m still getting through the day, still going to work, still doing what I’m supposed to do, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAt work, it’s the little stuff that adds up. I’ve had dispatch give me details — like timing, addresses, what the customer said — and then I have to ask them to repeat it because it just didn’t stick the way it normally would. Same with clients sometimes, and I hate that because I don’t want them thinking I’m careless or not paying attention. And then on the sales side, I’ve had a couple follow-ups slip through because I had too many ideas or tasks bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call that person back,” and then something else would grab my attention, and then later I’d realize I never did it. That affects how I feel about myself, because I’ve always tried to be dependable.\n\nAt home it’s probably the part that bothers me more. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least I can tell they’re being careful. Like they’re trying not to add pressure or say the wrong thing. And that makes the house feel strained. I’m supposed to be helping, you know, keeping things steady, not being another source of tension.\n\nSo yeah, my overall quality of life is affected. Not like I can’t function, but things feel harder, and I don’t feel as easy to be around as I’d like to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home as much as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it happens in both places, but it shows up a little differently. At work it’s more obvious because there are consequences right away — like I’m driving, I hear every CB chirp, I notice every fuel price sign, some new idea pops into my head, and then suddenly I’ve missed a detail I normally wouldn’t miss. Like a client’s delivery note, or who I promised I’d call back. That’s honestly part of what finally got me in here, because over these months it’s been happening enough that I can’t just brush it off.\n\nAt home, it’s not like delivery notes or dispatch instructions, but my mind still jumps around. I’ll be trying to listen or do one thing, and then I’m pulled off by something else in my head or around me. So yes, it’s there at home too — maybe work makes it stand out more because there are so many moving parts and little details I’m supposed to track. But it’s not only a work thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I haven’t cut my hours or had my responsibilities taken away or anything like that. I’m still doing the driving and the sales side of things.\n\nBut I’ll be honest, it’s made the work feel harder than it should. I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat details I normally would’ve tracked the first time — addresses, timing, little instructions — and that bothers me because I take pride in being reliable. Same with clients, there’ve been moments where I’m thinking, “Come on, you should’ve caught that,” and then I have to ask again.\n\nAnd a couple of sales follow-ups slipped through because I had too many things bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call this person back,” then another idea or task would jump in, and by the time I circled back, I’d missed it or delayed it. So no, I haven’t reduced my workload formally, but I can feel myself needing more effort just to keep up.\n\nAt home it’s kind of similar in a different way. My wife and daughter haven’t told me, “You need to do less,” exactly, but I can tell they’re walking on eggshells sometimes, like they’re trying not to add to my stress. That makes me feel bad, because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady, not making the house feel tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily activities or overall quality of life due to these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, though I’d say it’s more of a steady wearing-down kind of impact than one big dramatic thing. I’m still getting through the day, still going to work, still doing what I’m supposed to do, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAt work, it’s the little stuff that adds up. I’ve had dispatch give me details — like timing, addresses, what the customer said — and then I have to ask them to repeat it because it just didn’t stick the way it normally would. Same with clients sometimes, and I hate that because I don’t want them thinking I’m careless or not paying attention. And then on the sales side, I’ve had a couple follow-ups slip through because I had too many ideas or tasks bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call that person back,” and then something else would grab my attention, and then later I’d realize I never did it. That affects how I feel about myself, because I’ve always tried to be dependable.\n\nAt home it’s probably the part that bothers me more. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least I can tell they’re being careful. Like they’re trying not to add pressure or say the wrong thing. Not like I can’t function, but things feel harder, and I don’t feel as easy to be around as I’d like to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home as much as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it happens in both places, but it shows up a little differently. At work it’s more obvious because there are consequences right away — like I’m driving, I hear every CB chirp, I notice every fuel price sign, some new idea pops into my head, and then suddenly I’ve missed a detail I normally wouldn’t miss. Like a client’s delivery note, or who I promised I’d call back. That’s honestly part of what finally got me in here, because over these months it’s been happening enough that I can’t just brush it off.\n\nAt home, it’s not like delivery notes or dispatch instructions, but my mind still jumps around. I’ll be trying to listen or do one thing, and then I’m pulled off by something else in my head or around me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. At home it’s maybe a little less obvious than at work, because there isn’t always a dispatch call or a customer waiting on me, but it still happens.

Like if I’m trying to sit down and relax, watch something, or just spend time at home, my attention doesn’t stay put the way it should. My mind keeps hopping — one thought to another, or I’ll notice some little thing and then I’m off on that. It’s the same kind of pattern that’s been happening on the road, where every CB chirp or fuel price sign or random new idea grabs me, and then I realize I’ve missed something I normally wouldn’t miss.

That’s really what finally pushed me to come in. Over these months, it’s not just been a one-off thing. I’ll miss details I usually pride myself on catching — like a client’s delivery note, or who I promised I was going to call back. And at home, even if the stakes are lower, it still feels like my brain won’t just settle into one thing. I can be physically sitting there trying to relax, but mentally I’m bouncing around.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. I’m here because… well, what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that lately my mind just will not stay on one thing. I drive for work, deliveries and sales calls, that kind of thing, and over these past months it’s like every little thing grabs me — the CB crackling, a fuel price sign changing, some new idea popping into my head — and then I’m off track.\n\nAnd I’m not usually like that, or at least not to where it causes problems. I’ve been missing details I normally catch, like a client’s delivery note, or I’ll forget who I told I’d call back. Then I’m sitting there later trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s frustrating because I’ve done this work a long time. It’s not like I don’t know how to keep track of things. My attention just keeps jumping around, and that’s what got me worried enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety, like you're constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m pacing around every second, but I definitely have this… antsy feeling if I’m just sitting too long. Like if I’m home and there’s nothing I’m supposed to be doing, I’ll suddenly decide the garage needs fixing up, or something around the house has to be tightened or cleaned or reorganized right then.\n\nAnd with work, I keep adding things for myself that probably don’t need to be done. I’ll rewrite my route plan even after it’s already fine, or check in on newer drivers after hours, like I’ve appointed myself to make sure everything’s running right. It feels productive at the time, but it’s also like I can’t just leave things alone. If I sit still, I start feeling keyed up, like I need to get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it impulsive, exactly. I mean, I can usually think things through when it matters, like with driving or dealing with customers. I’m not out there just doing wild things without any thought.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump into something around the house or at work before really sitting down and deciding if it needs doing, but that feels more like I’m already keyed up and I just start moving, if that makes sense. Like I’ll decide something has to be handled right then, and later I might think, “Well, that could’ve waited.” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by acting on impulse. I haven’t really noticed myself making big reckless decisions or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that, honestly. I mean, I can talk if I’m comfortable with people, like at work or with family, and sometimes I probably explain things more than I need to — my wife has told me before I can take the long way around a story — but I wouldn’t say I’m always the one dominating a conversation or talking over everybody.\n\nIn social situations, it kind of depends who I’m with. If it’s people I know, I’ll join in, sure. If it’s people I don’t know well, I’m not usually trying to be the center of attention or anything. So… I guess I’m not sure I’d say “more than others,” no. At least it’s not something that’s stood out to me as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that people have pointed out to me, anyway. I mean, everybody cuts in now and then when a conversation’s moving fast, especially at work when there’s a schedule or someone’s trying to explain a route and I already know the part they’re getting to. I might jump in with, “Yeah, yeah, I’ve got it,” or ask a question before they’re totally finished, but I don’t think I’m constantly interrupting people.\n\nMy wife might say I get ahead of myself sometimes, like I think I know what she’s about to say and I answer that instead of letting her finish. But I wouldn’t describe it as me barging over people all the time or not letting anyone talk. It’s more… if I’m interested or I think there’s something that needs to be clarified, I might come in a little early. But I haven’t really noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your attention and feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about seven months now, give or take. It started around the time my daughter moved back home, and I was trying to keep everything steady — the house, work, everybody’s routine, all of that. At first I thought it was just stress from the change, like I had more on my plate and needed to stay on top of things, but it’s kind of kept going.\n\nSo… yeah, roughly seven months. It wasn’t something I noticed overnight, more like I looked back and realized I’d been feeling keyed up and having trouble keeping my attention where it needed to be for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with your attention and restlessness been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say no, but honestly it has started to cause some issues. Not like I’ve blown up my job or anything, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nAt work, with driving and sales, there are a lot of little details you’re supposed to keep straight — addresses, delivery notes, what dispatch said, what a client asked for, who needed a call back, that kind of thing. Lately I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat things I really should’ve caught the first time, and sometimes clients too, which is embarrassing because I don’t want to come across like I’m not listening. A couple of sales follow-ups slipped through the cracks because I was trying to chase too many things at once in my head, like I’d think of one idea, then another, then I’d tell myself I’d circle back… and then I didn’t.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s causing strain. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least that’s how it feels. I can see them measuring what they say or trying not to set me off or add to my stress, and that makes me feel awful because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady at home, not making everybody tense. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting both work and relationships some. Not completely out of control, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to reduce your work hours or responsibilities due to these difficulties with attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not officially, no. I haven’t cut my hours or had my responsibilities taken away or anything like that. I’m still doing the driving and the sales side of things.\n\nBut I’ll be honest, it’s made the work feel harder than it should. My wife and daughter haven’t told me, “You need to do less,” exactly, but I can tell they’re walking on eggshells sometimes, like they’re trying not to add to my stress. That makes me feel bad, because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady, not making the house feel tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily activities or overall quality of life due to these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, though I’d say it’s more of a steady wearing-down kind of impact than one big dramatic thing. I’m still getting through the day, still going to work, still doing what I’m supposed to do, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAt work, it’s the little stuff that adds up. I’ve had dispatch give me details — like timing, addresses, what the customer said — and then I have to ask them to repeat it because it just didn’t stick the way it normally would. Same with clients sometimes, and I hate that because I don’t want them thinking I’m careless or not paying attention. And then on the sales side, I’ve had a couple follow-ups slip through because I had too many ideas or tasks bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call that person back,” and then something else would grab my attention, and then later I’d realize I never did it. That affects how I feel about myself, because I’ve always tried to be dependable.\n\nAt home it’s probably the part that bothers me more. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least I can tell they’re being careful. Like they’re trying not to add pressure or say the wrong thing. And that makes the house feel strained. I’m supposed to be helping, you know, keeping things steady, not being another source of tension.\n\nSo yeah, my overall quality of life is affected. Not like I can’t function, but things feel harder, and I don’t feel as easy to be around as I’d like to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home as much as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it happens in both places, but it shows up a little differently. At work it’s more obvious because there are consequences right away — like I’m driving, I hear every CB chirp, I notice every fuel price sign, some new idea pops into my head, and then suddenly I’ve missed a detail I normally wouldn’t miss. Like a client’s delivery note, or who I promised I’d call back. That’s honestly part of what finally got me in here, because over these months it’s been happening enough that I can’t just brush it off.\n\nAt home, it’s not like delivery notes or dispatch instructions, but my mind still jumps around. I’ll be trying to listen or do one thing, and then I’m pulled off by something else in my head or around me. So yes, it’s there at home too — maybe work makes it stand out more because there are so many moving parts and little details I’m supposed to track. But it’s not only a work thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus on tasks during leisure activities or when you're relaxing at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. At home it’s maybe a little less obvious than at work, because there isn’t always a dispatch call or a customer waiting on me, but it still happens.\n\nLike if I’m trying to sit down and relax, watch something, or just spend time at home, my attention doesn’t stay put the way it should. My mind keeps hopping — one thought to another, or I’ll notice some little thing and then I’m off on that. It’s the same kind of pattern that’s been happening on the road, where every CB chirp or fuel price sign or random new idea grabs me, and then I realize I’ve missed something I normally wouldn’t miss.\n\nThat’s really what finally pushed me to come in. Over these months, it’s not just been a one-off thing. I’ll miss details I usually pride myself on catching — like a client’s delivery note, or who I promised I was going to call back. And at home, even if the stakes are lower, it still feels like my brain won’t just settle into one thing. I can be physically sitting there trying to relax, but mentally I’m bouncing around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, it’s been at least six months. I’d put it at around seven months, give or take.

I’ve been missing details I normally catch, like a client’s delivery note, or I’ll forget who I told I’d call back. Then I’m sitting there later trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s frustrating because I’ve done this work a long time. It’s not like I don’t know how to keep track of things. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m pacing around every second, but I definitely have this… antsy feeling if I’m just sitting too long. If I sit still, I start feeling keyed up, like I need to get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it impulsive, exactly. I mean, I can usually think things through when it matters, like with driving or dealing with customers. I’m not out there just doing wild things without any thought.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump into something around the house or at work before really sitting down and deciding if it needs doing, but that feels more like I’m already keyed up and I just start moving, if that makes sense. Like I’ll decide something has to be handled right then, and later I might think, “Well, that could’ve waited.” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by acting on impulse. I haven’t really noticed myself making big reckless decisions or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that, honestly. I mean, I can talk if I’m comfortable with people, like at work or with family, and sometimes I probably explain things more than I need to — my wife has told me before I can take the long way around a story — but I wouldn’t say I’m always the one dominating a conversation or talking over everybody.\n\nIn social situations, it kind of depends who I’m with. If it’s people I know, I’ll join in, sure. If it’s people I don’t know well, I’m not usually trying to be the center of attention or anything. So… I guess I’m not sure I’d say “more than others,” no. At least it’s not something that’s stood out to me as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that people have pointed out to me, anyway. I mean, everybody cuts in now and then when a conversation’s moving fast, especially at work when there’s a schedule or someone’s trying to explain a route and I already know the part they’re getting to. I might jump in with, “Yeah, yeah, I’ve got it,” or ask a question before they’re totally finished, but I don’t think I’m constantly interrupting people.\n\nMy wife might say I get ahead of myself sometimes, like I think I know what she’s about to say and I answer that instead of letting her finish. But I wouldn’t describe it as me barging over people all the time or not letting anyone talk. It’s more… if I’m interested or I think there’s something that needs to be clarified, I might come in a little early. But I haven’t really noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your attention and feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about seven months now, give or take. It started around the time my daughter moved back home, and I was trying to keep everything steady — the house, work, everybody’s routine, all of that. At first I thought it was just stress from the change, like I had more on my plate and needed to stay on top of things, but it’s kind of kept going.\n\nSo… yeah, roughly seven months. It wasn’t something I noticed overnight, more like I looked back and realized I’d been feeling keyed up and having trouble keeping my attention where it needed to be for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with your attention and restlessness been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say no, but honestly it has started to cause some issues. Not like I’ve blown up my job or anything, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nAt work, with driving and sales, there are a lot of little details you’re supposed to keep straight — addresses, delivery notes, what dispatch said, what a client asked for, who needed a call back, that kind of thing. Lately I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat things I really should’ve caught the first time, and sometimes clients too, which is embarrassing because I don’t want to come across like I’m not listening. A couple of sales follow-ups slipped through the cracks because I was trying to chase too many things at once in my head, like I’d think of one idea, then another, then I’d tell myself I’d circle back… and then I didn’t.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s causing strain. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least that’s how it feels. I can see them measuring what they say or trying not to set me off or add to my stress, and that makes me feel awful because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady at home, not making everybody tense. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting both work and relationships some. Not completely out of control, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to reduce your work hours or responsibilities due to these difficulties with attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not officially, no. I haven’t cut my hours or had my responsibilities taken away or anything like that. I’m still doing the driving and the sales side of things.\n\nBut I’ll be honest, it’s made the work feel harder than it should. I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat details I normally would’ve tracked the first time — addresses, timing, little instructions — and that bothers me because I take pride in being reliable. Same with clients, there’ve been moments where I’m thinking, “Come on, you should’ve caught that,” and then I have to ask again.\n\nAnd a couple of sales follow-ups slipped through because I had too many things bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call this person back,” then another idea or task would jump in, and by the time I circled back, I’d missed it or delayed it. So no, I haven’t reduced my workload formally, but I can feel myself needing more effort just to keep up.\n\nAt home it’s kind of similar in a different way. My wife and daughter haven’t told me, “You need to do less,” exactly, but I can tell they’re walking on eggshells sometimes, like they’re trying not to add to my stress. That makes me feel bad, because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady, not making the house feel tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily activities or overall quality of life due to these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, though I’d say it’s more of a steady wearing-down kind of impact than one big dramatic thing. I’m still getting through the day, still going to work, still doing what I’m supposed to do, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAt work, it’s the little stuff that adds up. I’ve had dispatch give me details — like timing, addresses, what the customer said — and then I have to ask them to repeat it because it just didn’t stick the way it normally would. Same with clients sometimes, and I hate that because I don’t want them thinking I’m careless or not paying attention. And then on the sales side, I’ve had a couple follow-ups slip through because I had too many ideas or tasks bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call that person back,” and then something else would grab my attention, and then later I’d realize I never did it. That affects how I feel about myself, because I’ve always tried to be dependable.\n\nAt home it’s probably the part that bothers me more. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least I can tell they’re being careful. Like they’re trying not to add pressure or say the wrong thing. And that makes the house feel strained. I’m supposed to be helping, you know, keeping things steady, not being another source of tension.\n\nSo yeah, my overall quality of life is affected. Not like I can’t function, but things feel harder, and I don’t feel as easy to be around as I’d like to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home as much as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it happens in both places, but it shows up a little differently. ]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been there pretty much the whole time, yeah — about seven months, since my daughter moved back home and I started feeling like I had to keep everything steady.

I wouldn’t say every single day has been exactly the same. Some days it’s more noticeable than others, depending on what’s going on or how much I’m trying to juggle. But it hasn’t really gone away during that stretch. It’s more like it became part of the background — the restlessness, the trouble keeping my attention where it needs to be — and then certain days it sticks out more.

So, yeah, the severity has varied some, but the problem itself has been pretty consistent over those seven months.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. I’m here because… well, what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that lately my mind just will not stay on one thing. I drive for work, deliveries and sales calls, that kind of thing, and over these past months it’s like every little thing grabs me — the CB crackling, a fuel price sign changing, some new idea popping into my head — and then I’m off track.\n\nAnd I’m not usually like that, or at least not to where it causes problems. I’ve been missing details I normally catch, like a client’s delivery note, or I’ll forget who I told I’d call back. Then I’m sitting there later trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s frustrating because I’ve done this work a long time. It’s not like I don’t know how to keep track of things. My attention just keeps jumping around, and that’s what got me worried enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety, like you're constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m pacing around every second, but I definitely have this… antsy feeling if I’m just sitting too long. Like if I’m home and there’s nothing I’m supposed to be doing, I’ll suddenly decide the garage needs fixing up, or something around the house has to be tightened or cleaned or reorganized right then.\n\nAnd with work, I keep adding things for myself that probably don’t need to be done. I’ll rewrite my route plan even after it’s already fine, or check in on newer drivers after hours, like I’ve appointed myself to make sure everything’s running right. It feels productive at the time, but it’s also like I can’t just leave things alone. If I sit still, I start feeling keyed up, like I need to get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it impulsive, exactly. I mean, I can usually think things through when it matters, like with driving or dealing with customers. I’m not out there just doing wild things without any thought.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump into something around the house or at work before really sitting down and deciding if it needs doing, but that feels more like I’m already keyed up and I just start moving, if that makes sense. Like I’ll decide something has to be handled right then, and later I might think, “Well, that could’ve waited.” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by acting on impulse. I haven’t really noticed myself making big reckless decisions or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that, honestly. I mean, I can talk if I’m comfortable with people, like at work or with family, and sometimes I probably explain things more than I need to — my wife has told me before I can take the long way around a story — but I wouldn’t say I’m always the one dominating a conversation or talking over everybody.\n\nIn social situations, it kind of depends who I’m with. If it’s people I know, I’ll join in, sure. If it’s people I don’t know well, I’m not usually trying to be the center of attention or anything. So… I guess I’m not sure I’d say “more than others,” no. At least it’s not something that’s stood out to me as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that people have pointed out to me, anyway. I mean, everybody cuts in now and then when a conversation’s moving fast, especially at work when there’s a schedule or someone’s trying to explain a route and I already know the part they’re getting to. I might jump in with, “Yeah, yeah, I’ve got it,” or ask a question before they’re totally finished, but I don’t think I’m constantly interrupting people.\n\nMy wife might say I get ahead of myself sometimes, like I think I know what she’s about to say and I answer that instead of letting her finish. But I wouldn’t describe it as me barging over people all the time or not letting anyone talk. It’s more… if I’m interested or I think there’s something that needs to be clarified, I might come in a little early. But I haven’t really noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your attention and feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about seven months now, give or take. It started around the time my daughter moved back home, and I was trying to keep everything steady — the house, work, everybody’s routine, all of that. At first I thought it was just stress from the change, like I had more on my plate and needed to stay on top of things, but it’s kind of kept going.\n\nSo… yeah, roughly seven months. It wasn’t something I noticed overnight, more like I looked back and realized I’d been feeling keyed up and having trouble keeping my attention where it needed to be for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with your attention and restlessness been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say no, but honestly it has started to cause some issues. Not like I’ve blown up my job or anything, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nAt work, with driving and sales, there are a lot of little details you’re supposed to keep straight — addresses, delivery notes, what dispatch said, what a client asked for, who needed a call back, that kind of thing. Lately I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat things I really should’ve caught the first time, and sometimes clients too, which is embarrassing because I don’t want to come across like I’m not listening. A couple of sales follow-ups slipped through the cracks because I was trying to chase too many things at once in my head, like I’d think of one idea, then another, then I’d tell myself I’d circle back… and then I didn’t.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s causing strain. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least that’s how it feels. I can see them measuring what they say or trying not to set me off or add to my stress, and that makes me feel awful because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady at home, not making everybody tense. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting both work and relationships some. Not completely out of control, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to reduce your work hours or responsibilities due to these difficulties with attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not officially, no. I haven’t cut my hours or had my responsibilities taken away or anything like that. I’m still doing the driving and the sales side of things.\n\nBut I’ll be honest, it’s made the work feel harder than it should. I’ve had to ask dispatch to repeat details I normally would’ve tracked the first time — addresses, timing, little instructions — and that bothers me because I take pride in being reliable. Same with clients, there’ve been moments where I’m thinking, “Come on, you should’ve caught that,” and then I have to ask again.\n\nAnd a couple of sales follow-ups slipped through because I had too many things bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call this person back,” then another idea or task would jump in, and by the time I circled back, I’d missed it or delayed it. So no, I haven’t reduced my workload formally, but I can feel myself needing more effort just to keep up.\n\nAt home it’s kind of similar in a different way. My wife and daughter haven’t told me, “You need to do less,” exactly, but I can tell they’re walking on eggshells sometimes, like they’re trying not to add to my stress. That makes me feel bad, because I’m supposed to be helping keep things steady, not making the house feel tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily activities or overall quality of life due to these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, though I’d say it’s more of a steady wearing-down kind of impact than one big dramatic thing. I’m still getting through the day, still going to work, still doing what I’m supposed to do, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAt work, it’s the little stuff that adds up. I’ve had dispatch give me details — like timing, addresses, what the customer said — and then I have to ask them to repeat it because it just didn’t stick the way it normally would. Same with clients sometimes, and I hate that because I don’t want them thinking I’m careless or not paying attention. And then on the sales side, I’ve had a couple follow-ups slip through because I had too many ideas or tasks bouncing around in my head at once. I’d think, “Okay, I need to call that person back,” and then something else would grab my attention, and then later I’d realize I never did it. That affects how I feel about myself, because I’ve always tried to be dependable.\n\nAt home it’s probably the part that bothers me more. My wife and my daughter have been kind of walking on eggshells around me, or at least I can tell they’re being careful. Like they’re trying not to add pressure or say the wrong thing. And that makes the house feel strained. I’m supposed to be helping, you know, keeping things steady, not being another source of tension.\n\nSo yeah, my overall quality of life is affected. Not like I can’t function, but things feel harder, and I don’t feel as easy to be around as I’d like to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home as much as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it happens in both places, but it shows up a little differently. At work it’s more obvious because there are consequences right away — like I’m driving, I hear every CB chirp, I notice every fuel price sign, some new idea pops into my head, and then suddenly I’ve missed a detail I normally wouldn’t miss. Like a client’s delivery note, or who I promised I’d call back. That’s honestly part of what finally got me in here, because over these months it’s been happening enough that I can’t just brush it off.\n\nAt home, it’s not like delivery notes or dispatch instructions, but my mind still jumps around. I’ll be trying to listen or do one thing, and then I’m pulled off by something else in my head or around me. So yes, it’s there at home too — maybe work makes it stand out more because there are so many moving parts and little details I’m supposed to track. But it’s not only a work thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus on tasks during leisure activities or when you're relaxing at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. At home it’s maybe a little less obvious than at work, because there isn’t always a dispatch call or a customer waiting on me, but it still happens.\n\nLike if I’m trying to sit down and relax, watch something, or just spend time at home, my attention doesn’t stay put the way it should. My mind keeps hopping — one thought to another, or I’ll notice some little thing and then I’m off on that. It’s the same kind of pattern that’s been happening on the road, where every CB chirp or fuel price sign or random new idea grabs me, and then I realize I’ve missed something I normally wouldn’t miss.\n\nThat’s really what finally pushed me to come in. Over these months, it’s not just been a one-off thing. I’ll miss details I usually pride myself on catching — like a client’s delivery note, or who I promised I was going to call back. And at home, even if the stakes are lower, it still feels like my brain won’t just settle into one thing. I can be physically sitting there trying to relax, but mentally I’m bouncing around.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been at least six months. I’d put it at around seven months, give or take.\n\nI remember it starting to really become noticeable after my daughter moved back home. That kind of changed the whole feel of the house, not in a bad way exactly, but it added this feeling like I needed to keep everything steady and make sure everybody was okay. And since then, these issues have just sort of… stayed there. It hasn’t been just a rough week or a bad month. It’s been going on long enough now that I can’t really explain it away as being tired or having a lot on my plate. So yeah, about seven months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present and consistent over the entire seven months, or have they varied in severity over that time?\n\nPatient: It’s been there pretty much the whole time, yeah — about seven months, since my daughter moved back home and I started feeling like I had to keep everything steady.\n\nI wouldn’t say every single day has been exactly the same. Some days it’s more noticeable than others, depending on what’s going on or how much I’m trying to juggle. But it hasn’t really gone away during that stretch. drivers after hours, fixing things around the house—and I feel antsy if I sit still too long.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I got it in my head that I could rebuild the whole driver training program myself and make it run better than the bosses ever could, even though that’s not really my lane.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: work I’ve had to have dispatch and clients repeat details I should have tracked, and a couple of my sales follow-ups slipped through because I was chasing too many ideas at once. At home, my wife and daughter have been walking on eggshells, and I can tell I’m adding strain when I’m supposed to be helping.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since my daughter moved back home and I started trying to keep everything steady.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 50 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus on tasks during leisure activities or when you're relaxing at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. My mind keeps hopping — one thought to another, or I’ll notice some little thing and then I’m off on that. It’s the same kind of pattern that’s been happening on the road, where every CB chirp or fuel price sign or random new idea grabs me, and then I realize I’ve missed something I normally wouldn’t miss.\n\nThat’s really what finally pushed me to come in. Over these months, it’s not just been a one-off thing. I’ll miss details I usually pride myself on catching — like a client’s delivery note, or who I promised I was going to call back. And at home, even if the stakes are lower, it still feels like my brain won’t just settle into one thing. And since then, these issues have just sort of… stayed there. It hasn’t been just a rough week or a bad month. It’s been going on long enough now that I can’t really explain it away as being tired or having a lot on my plate. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. I’m here because… well, what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that lately my mind just will not stay on one thing. I’ve been missing details I normally catch, like a client’s delivery note, or I’ll forget who I told I’d call back.
